## Feature slice 1 obligations

Feature slice 1 is the first slice after the port, and it retires a falsehood the run's own scoring prints. The gate runner answers one integer for a gate it ran, and the same spelling, minus one, stands for a gate that exited with no code the runner could read, for a gate that could not be started, and for a gate the runner killed at its timeout, and the row judgement reads any nonzero planted spelling as a catch. A gate that never finished therefore earns the caught cell for a defect it never judged. The owner probe against the crate at the Round 19 head establishes the falsehood end to end: one synthetic target whose gate sleeps three seconds where the inverted comparison's marker stands in the planted file and exits at once where it does not, run at a one second timeout, scores seed-02 caught, where the gate rendered no judgement at all and was killed at its deadline, and scores the neighbouring seeds waved through on the same run, their gates exiting clean. Where a feature slice 1 obligation and an earlier obligation disagree, the feature slice 1 obligation governs.

Obligation F1-1 covers the gate outcome reading. The runner distinguishes a gate that exited, answering its code as it answers today, from a gate it killed at its timeout, and that distinction alone is new. A gate that cannot be started keeps its standing spelling and its standing judgements, because a harness that cannot start its gate is a broken harness and not a slow one, and this slice does not move it.

Obligation F1-2 covers the planted cell. A planted gate the runner killed at its timeout earns the cell word timed-out, and never the word caught. A planted gate that exits nonzero still earns caught, and a planted gate that exits zero still earns waved-through, exactly as the standing reading judges them.

Obligation F1-3 covers the clean side and the recorded spellings. A clean gate the runner killed at its timeout keeps the gate-broken standing its nonzero spelling earns today, because a gate that cannot finish on the clean tree earns no catches in any case and the word already says so. The recorded exit spellings change nowhere: the clean exits and the planted exits keep their integers in the result document, a timeout still spelling minus one in both, and the standing pin on the clean exit line stands untouched.

Obligation F1-4 covers the summaries and the renderings. The per gate summary keeps its four standing keys, and a timed-out cell is counted under none of them, so no standing rendering changes shape and no recorded answer moves. The cell word itself stands in the row's gates in the result document and in both renderings exactly as every cell word stands, and a reader of the table sees the timeout where it happened and sees the gate's counts sum short of its rows by exactly the timed-out cells.

Obligation F1-5 covers the unchanged past and the proof. Every standing battery replays unaltered and the committed example reports regenerate byte identical, because no standing recording holds a gate that timed out. The behaviours of this slice are proven by the pins in tests/feature1_pins.rs named test_planted_gate_killed_at_its_timeout_reads_timed_out, test_planted_gate_exiting_nonzero_still_reads_caught, and test_clean_gate_killed_at_its_timeout_still_reads_gate_broken, each driving the run over a synthetic target in the open.

## Feature slice 2 obligations

Feature slice 2 grows the seed registry a tenth time, and the pack grows an eleventh format version to hold it. Six candidate families were weighed for the four new seeds, and two of them were set aside, and the reasons belong in this record the way the earlier records hold theirs. The dropped try else clause stands first in the weighing because slice 14 set it aside for one reason alone, that no seed in the registry read the else clause of a loop at all and the loop clause was the one to take first, and slice 14 has since taken the loop clause as seed-44, so the reason is spent and the try clause stands as the reserve it was left as. Swapped dictionary entry values, exchanging the values of the first two keyed entries of a dictionary literal, was set aside again on the ground slice 14 stated, which stands unspent: the dictionary literal already carries two plantings, seed-09 removing its first entry and seed-17 exchanging the key and the value inside every entry, and a third planting on that one node crowds it while whole families stand unplanted elsewhere, four of which this slice takes. A renamed keyword argument, spelling a call's named argument with a different name, was set aside because the name it would spell has to be a parameter the callee actually holds, which is a fact about a definition standing somewhere else in the tree or outside it, and the collectors of this registry read the call node as it stands and never resolve its target to a definition, so the seed would plant renames the language refuses or bindings nothing reads, the decidability ground slice 9 stated for the families it set aside. The probes were run against the crate at the Round 20 head, and their findings stand here. No apply function reads the else clause of a try statement: the readers that touch the try node are the handler reader of seed-36 and the finally reader of seed-34, and the else readers of the registry are the if reader of seed-13 and the loop readers of seed-44, each on its own statement. The slice readers of seed-29 and seed-40 read the upper and lower fields of the slice node and never its step, and no seed reads the step at all. No apply function exchanges the two arms of a conditional expression, the only readers naming that node being the test boundary readers that seed-47's ground states, which read its test and never its arms. No apply function reads the cause of a raise, which stands in the tree's field table and in no collector. Removing the else clause of a try statement compiles on the plain shapes, and the one conjunction that refuses is the standing shape the loop else seed already pins at the write path, an else clause holding the only binding for a nonlocal declaration standing in a nested function. The PORT2 battery iterates every seed of the registry over every one of its cases, so the grown registry's entries for the four new seeds in that battery are recorded by an independent implementation of the four obligations below, written in Python over its ast module from this section and standing in the round's scratch, because the Python the earlier batteries recorded against stands retired and a recording taken from the port itself would prove nothing. The four families taken are the dropped try else clause, the shifted slice step, the swapped conditional expression arms, and the dropped exception chain, in that order, taking the ids seed-48 through seed-51. The obligations below amend R-1 and R-6 and the version ranges of J-1 and J-2 where they conflict, and the amendments are stated here rather than left as silent conflicts between the texts. Where a feature slice 2 obligation and an earlier obligation disagree, the feature slice 2 obligation governs.

Obligation F2-1 covers the grown registry. The registry holds fifty two seeds with unique stable ids running in order from seed-00 to seed-51. The original forty eight seeds keep their ids and their names and defect classes and descriptions and apply behaviour exactly as the earlier slices left them, and the four new seeds take the next four ids. This obligation amends R-1, which pinned the registry at exactly forty eight. Proven by test_registry_has_fifty_two_unique_seeds and test_seed_zero_is_the_stale_version_string and test_seed_metadata_is_complete, and by the PORT2 battery replaying whole with the four new seeds' entries standing in every case beside the forty eight whose entries do not move.

Obligation F2-2 covers seed-48, dropped try else clause. The seed plants at every try statement, starred or not, that carries an else clause by removing the whole clause, leaving the body of the try and its handlers and its finally clause standing exactly as written. A try standing in the body of another try is planted at its own level as well under the reading H-3 gives, and the body of the outer try stands as written apart from the plantings standing in it. The else clause of a try is the path the code wrote for nothing going wrong, the success path a fallible block falls through to, so removing it makes that path silently never run and the code after the statement meets whatever the try left standing instead. A try carrying no else clause is never planted, because it holds nothing this family removes. A try standing in the else clause of another statement is detached with that clause when the outer planting removes it, and the written file shows the outer planting alone, under the reading H-3 gives every seed. This seed is distinct from seed-34, which drops the body of a finally clause and never reads an else clause, and from seed-36, which drops an exception handler whole, and from seed-13, which removes the else branch of an if statement, and from seed-44, which removes the else clause of a loop and never reads a try statement. The matches are collected from the tree as parsed before any change is made, under the discipline of H-2 and H-3. Obligation F2-3 covers seed-49, shifted slice step. The seed plants at every slice whose step stands as an integer constant, under the same constant reading the bound seeds use, by raising that constant by one, leaving the slice's bounds and the expression it slices standing exactly as written. The stride of the slice now skips past values the code wrote it to land on, or lands on values between the ones it wrote, while the slice still reads as an ordinary slice of the same bounds. A slice carrying no step is never planted, because it holds no step node to raise and its stride is the language's own. Obligation F2-4 covers seed-50, swapped conditional expression arms. The seed plants at every conditional expression by exchanging the expression it yields when its test holds with the expression it yields when its test fails, leaving the test standing exactly as written. A raise carrying no cause, and a bare raise that names nothing at all, are never planted, because they hold nothing this family removes. The probes were run against the crate at the Round 21 head, and their findings stand here. The bound readers of seed-29 and seed-40 raise a slice's bounds and the step reader of seed-49 raises its step, and no apply function removes a bound of a slice or clears one to the language's open end. The one reader of the augmented assignment node is seed-24's, which reads its operator alone and exchanges it, and no apply function reads the node as the plain assignment it stands beside. The one reader of a definition's arguments node among the applies is seed-33's, which reads the defaults and the keyword defaults and exchanges two of them, and no apply function exchanges the parameters themselves. The only readers naming the import node are the binding collectors, which record the names an import binds and plant nothing, and no seed plants on an import statement at all. One family returns after four rounds set it aside, and it returns because the ground that set it aside is now answered in writing. Swapped dictionary entry values was set aside in slice 14 and again in feature slices 2 and 3 and 4 on the ground slice 12 stated for the slice node, that the dictionary literal already carries two plantings and a third planting on that one node crowds it while whole families stand unplanted elsewhere. The registry's own practice has since answered that ground: the slice node now carries five plantings, seed-26 on its whole index form and seed-29 on its stop and seed-40 on its start and seed-49 on its step and seed-52 removing a bound, each a distinct change to a distinct aspect of the one node, and the weighing of a family in this registry has never counted plantings on a node but has asked whether the change planted is one the registry already plants. The probes were run against the crate at the Round 23 head, and their findings stand here. No apply function reads the assert node at all, the statement standing in no collector of the registry. The one reader of the list literal among the applies is seed-09's, which removes its first element and reads no tuple literal, and no apply function exchanges two elements of any literal. Seed-22 removes every unary minus wherever one stands, so a numeric constant already standing as the operand of a unary minus stands outside the mirror this slice takes, because wrapping that constant in a second minus plants the removal of the first by another spelling. At every break statement and every continue statement standing in the body of a loop, the statement stands removed whole, leaving every other statement of the body it stood in standing exactly as written apart from the plantings standing in them. At every try statement whose body and whose else body both stand written and nonempty, the two bodies stand exchanged whole, each keeping everything it carries, and the statement's handlers and its finally clause standing exactly as written apart from the plantings standing in them.
